No, not really other people, not in the same way. I mean, people may be going along with her, or they may be letting her explain things her way, and that upsets me because it makes me look like I’m the difficult one. But the person I came here about is my husband’s sister.

She’s the one who’s been working this family business, little by little, getting herself in closer to the money and the decisions, and pushing me out like I don’t have a say. And it’s not something that happened once, you know? It’s been over a year of small things, and then when I say something, folks try to smooth it over like, “Oh, that’s just how she is,” or “You’re reading too much into it.” But I know what I’ve been seeing.

So no, I’m not saying everybody is out to get me. I’m saying she is doing this deliberately, and other people just don’t want to admit it.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, not really. I mean, I wouldn’t say I’ve lost interest in people or stopped doing the things I usually do. I still go to my quilting group, and I still go to choir practice. I’ve been keeping up with my GED lessons too, and I do my senior walks like I normally do. Those things are important to me, and I haven’t just dropped them or anything.

What has changed is more around family, especially anything where my husband’s sister is going to be there. I’m more careful now. I don’t feel comfortable at every gathering the way I used to, because I don’t trust her, and I don’t like sitting there pretending everything is fine when I know what she’s been doing. So I might avoid certain family things, or I might not stay as long, depending on who’s there and what it’s about.

But that’s not me not wanting to be around anybody or not enjoying my life. I still do my regular activities. It’s mostly changed how much I trust her and which family gatherings feel comfortable for me.

She’s been working this whole family business—little by little, you know, not in a way everybody wants to admit—to get control of the money and push me out of decisions.\n\nPeople keep trying to smooth it over like I’m misunderstanding things, or like it’s just normal family tension, but I know what I’ve been seeing. This hasn’t been just one argument or one bad week. It’s been going on for more than a year now, and after a while you start putting the pieces together. I just feel like I’m the only one willing to say what’s actually happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe for certain that your sister-in-law is deliberately trying to take control of the family money and push you out?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, I do. I know how that sounds when you say it straight like that, but this isn’t me just having a feeling one afternoon or being upset over some little family disagreement.\n\nI came here because my husband’s sister has been working this whole family business in a way that lets her get closer and closer to the money, and at the same time I’m getting pushed out of decisions I should be part of. And everybody keeps trying to make it sound softer, like, “Oh, she didn’t mean it that way,” or “That’s just how families are,” but after more than a year of watching it, I know what I’ve been seeing. It’s deliberate. She knows exactly what she’s doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing voices when no one else is there?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Not voices like someone talking to me when nobody’s there, no. I mean, sometimes I’ll be thinking things over in my head, going back over conversations, but that’s just me thinking. I’m not hearing some voice in the room or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Has your sister-in-law done anything that felt impossible or beyond normal means, like influencing your thoughts or controlling events in a way ordinary people couldn’t?\n\nPatient: No, not like… magic or controlling my thoughts, if that’s what you mean. I’m not saying she has some strange power over me or over the house.\n\nWhat I’m saying is more ordinary than that, but that doesn’t make it less real. She’s been working this family business, bit by bit, getting herself closer to the money and making sure I’m left out of decisions I should be part of. I don’t think I was like that before, no. I mean, I’ve never been someone who just trusts everybody right away, but I think that’s normal, isn’t it? You use your judgment.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F60.0, F23, F20.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, I’m glad you came in today. What brings you in?\n\nPatient: Well… I’m here because I need somebody outside the family to understand what’s been going on. It’s my husband’s sister. She’s been working this whole family business—little by little, you know, not in a way everybody wants to admit—to get control of the money and push me out of decisions.\n\nPeople keep trying to smooth it over like I’m misunderstanding things, or like it’s just normal family tension, but I know what I’ve been seeing. This hasn’t been just one argument or one bad week. It’s been going on for more than a year now, and after a while you start putting the pieces together. I just feel like I’m the only one willing to say what’s actually happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe for certain that your sister-in-law is deliberately trying to take control of the family money and push you out?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, I do. I know how that sounds when you say it straight like that, but this isn’t me just having a feeling one afternoon or being upset over some little family disagreement.\n\nI came here because my husband’s sister has been working this whole family business in a way that lets her get closer and closer to the money, and at the same time I’m getting pushed out of decisions I should be part of. And everybody keeps trying to make it sound softer, like, “Oh, she didn’t mean it that way,” or “That’s just how families are,” but after more than a year of watching it, I know what I’ve been seeing. It’s deliberate. She knows exactly what she’s doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing voices when no one else is there?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Not voices like someone talking to me when nobody’s there, no. I mean, sometimes I’ll be thinking things over in my head, going back over conversations, but that’s just me thinking. I’m not hearing some voice in the room or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Has your sister-in-law done anything that felt impossible or beyond normal means, like influencing your thoughts or controlling events in a way ordinary people couldn’t?\n\nPatient: No, not like… magic or controlling my thoughts, if that’s what you mean. I’m not saying she has some strange power over me or over the house.\n\nWhat I’m saying is more ordinary than that, but that doesn’t make it less real. She’s been working this family business, bit by bit, getting herself closer to the money and making sure I’m left out of decisions I should be part of. And when I try to point it out, people act like I’m making too much of it, like it’s just a misunderstanding. But I’ve been watching this for over a year now. I know what I’ve been seeing. It’s not beyond normal means—it’s her being deliberate and sneaky.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period when you felt unusually energetic and needed much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you mean. I’ve had nights where I didn’t sleep well because I was worried or going over things in my head, but that’s not the same as feeling full of energy, you know?\n\nI’m 52, I get tired like anybody else. If I don’t sleep, I usually feel it the next day. I’m not up cleaning the whole house at three in the morning feeling wonderful or anything like that. I can get worked up when I’m upset, but I wouldn’t say I’ve had a period where I suddenly needed hardly any sleep and had all this extra energy.\n\nDoctor: Apart from your sister-in-law, do you feel that other people are also deliberately trying to harm you or work against you?\n\nPatient: No, not really other people, not in the same way. I mean, people may be going along with her, or they may be letting her explain things her way, and that upsets me because it makes me look like I’m the difficult one. But the person I came here about is my husband’s sister.\n\nShe’s the one who’s been working this family business, little by little, getting herself in closer to the money and the decisions, and pushing me out like I don’t have a say. And it’s not something that happened once, you know? It’s been over a year of small things, and then when I say something, folks try to smooth it over like, “Oh, that’s just how she is,” or “You’re reading too much into it.” But I know what I’ve been seeing.\n\nSo no, I’m not saying everybody is out to get me. I’m saying she is doing this deliberately, and other people just don’t want to admit it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself becoming much less interested in being around people or doing things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I wouldn’t say I’ve lost interest in people or stopped doing the things I usually do. I still go to my quilting group, and I still go to choir practice. I’ve been keeping up with my GED lessons too, and I do my senior walks like I normally do. Those things are important to me, and I haven’t just dropped them or anything.\n\nWhat has changed is more around family, especially anything where my husband’s sister is going to be there. I’m more careful now. I don’t feel comfortable at every gathering the way I used to, because I don’t trust her, and I don’t like sitting there pretending everything is fine when I know what she’s been doing. So I might avoid certain family things, or I might not stay as long, depending on who’s there and what it’s about.\n\nBut that’s not me not wanting to be around anybody or not enjoying my life. I still do my regular activities. It’s mostly changed how much I trust her and which family gatherings feel comfortable for me.\n\nDoctor: Before all this with your sister-in-law began, did you generally tend to distrust other people and suspect they had hidden motives?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I was like that before, no. I mean, I’ve never been someone who just trusts everybody right away, but I think that’s normal, isn’t it? You use your judgment. If somebody seems pushy or two-faced, you notice it. But I wasn’t walking around thinking people had hidden motives all the time.\n\nBefore this, I got along with people pretty well. I did my things, talked to people, went where I needed to go. I might have had little disagreements here and there, like anyone does, but I wasn’t sitting there studying everybody and thinking they were against me.\n\nThis has been different because of how it’s happened and how it keeps happening. So I wouldn’t say I generally distrusted people before. I’d say I was careful, maybe, but not suspicious of everybody.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

I just feel like I’m the only one willing to say what’s actually happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe for certain that your sister-in-law is deliberately trying to take control of the family money and push you out?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, I do. I know how that sounds when you say it straight like that, but this isn’t me just having a feeling one afternoon or being upset over some little family disagreement.\n\nI came here because my husband’s sister has been working this whole family business in a way that lets her get closer and closer to the money, and at the same time I’m getting pushed out of decisions I should be part of. And everybody keeps trying to make it sound softer, like, “Oh, she didn’t mean it that way,” or “That’s just how families are,” but after more than a year of watching it, I know what I’ve been seeing. It’s deliberate. She knows exactly what she’s doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing voices when no one else is there?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Not voices like someone talking to me when nobody’s there, no. I mean, sometimes I’ll be thinking things over in my head, going back over conversations, but that’s just me thinking. I’m not hearing some voice in the room or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Has your sister-in-law done anything that felt impossible or beyond normal means, like influencing your thoughts or controlling events in a way ordinary people couldn’t?\n\nPatient: No, not like… magic or controlling my thoughts, if that’s what you mean. I’m not saying she has some strange power over me or over the house.\n\nWhat I’m saying is more ordinary than that, but that doesn’t make it less real. She’s been working this family business, bit by bit, getting herself closer to the money and making sure I’m left out of decisions I should be part of. And when I try to point it out, people act like I’m making too much of it, like it’s just a misunderstanding. But I’ve been watching this for over a year now. I know what I’ve been seeing. It’s not beyond normal means—it’s her being deliberate and sneaky.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period when you felt unusually energetic and needed much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you mean. I’ve had nights where I didn’t sleep well because I was worried or going over things in my head, but that’s not the same as feeling full of energy, you know?\n\nI’m 52, I get tired like anybody else. If I don’t sleep, I usually feel it the next day. I’m not up cleaning the whole house at three in the morning feeling wonderful or anything like that. I can get worked up when I’m upset, but I wouldn’t say I’ve had a period where I suddenly needed hardly any sleep and had all this extra energy.\n\nDoctor: Apart from your sister-in-law, do you feel that other people are also deliberately trying to harm you or work against you?\n\nPatient: No, not really other people, not in the same way. I mean, people may be going along with her, or they may be letting her explain things her way, and that upsets me because it makes me look like I’m the difficult one. But the person I came here about is my husband’s sister.\n\nShe’s the one who’s been working this family business, little by little, getting herself in closer to the money and the decisions, and pushing me out like I don’t have a say. And it’s not something that happened once, you know? It’s been over a year of small things, and then when I say something, folks try to smooth it over like, “Oh, that’s just how she is,” or “You’re reading too much into it.” But I know what I’ve been seeing.\n\nSo no, I’m not saying everybody is out to get me. I’m saying she is doing this deliberately, and other people just don’t want to admit it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself becoming much less interested in being around people or doing things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I wouldn’t say I’ve lost interest in people or stopped doing the things I usually do. I still go to my quilting group, and I still go to choir practice. I’ve been keeping up with my GED lessons too, and I do my senior walks like I normally do. Those things are important to me, and I haven’t just dropped them or anything.\n\nWhat has changed is more around family, especially anything where my husband’s sister is going to be there. I’m more careful now. I don’t feel comfortable at every gathering the way I used to, because I don’t trust her, and I don’t like sitting there pretending everything is fine when I know what she’s been doing. So I might avoid certain family things, or I might not stay as long, depending on who’s there and what it’s about.\n\nBut that’s not me not wanting to be around anybody or not enjoying my life. I still do my regular activities. It’s mostly changed how much I trust her and which family gatherings feel comfortable for me.\n\nDoctor: Before all this with your sister-in-law began, did you generally tend to distrust other people and suspect they had hidden motives?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I was like that before, no. I mean, I’ve never been someone who just trusts everybody right away, but I think that’s normal, isn’t it? You use your judgment. If somebody seems pushy or two-faced, you notice it. But I wasn’t walking around thinking people had hidden motives all the time.\n\nBefore this, I got along with people pretty well. I did my things, talked to people, went where I needed to go. I might have had little disagreements here and there, like anyone does, but I wasn’t sitting there studying everybody and thinking they were against me.\n\nThis has been different because of how it’s happened and how it keeps happening. So I wouldn’t say I generally distrusted people before. I’d say I was careful, maybe, but not suspicious of everybody.\n\nDoctor: For how long have you been certain that your sister-in-law is deliberately trying to push you out and take control of the money?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s been about a year and two months now. It started back when the arguments with my husband’s sister began over the family care planning, and that’s when I first really saw what she was doing.\n\nAt first, I tried to tell myself maybe it was just a disagreement, maybe she was just being pushy, but over time it became clearer to me. It wasn’t just one argument or one bad day. It’s been going on since then, little by little, and by now I feel certain she’s been trying to push me out and get control over the money side of things.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

No, I don’t think people usually tell me that. I mean, sometimes my family might say I’m going on too long about something, but that’s different, I think. It’s deliberate. She knows exactly what she’s doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing voices when no one else is there?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Not voices like someone talking to me when nobody’s there, no. I mean, sometimes I’ll be thinking things over in my head, going back over conversations, but that’s just me thinking. I’m not hearing some voice in the room or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Has your sister-in-law done anything that felt impossible or beyond normal means, like influencing your thoughts or controlling events in a way ordinary people couldn’t?\n\nPatient: No, not like… magic or controlling my thoughts, if that’s what you mean. I’m not saying she has some strange power over me or over the house.\n\nWhat I’m saying is more ordinary than that, but that doesn’t make it less real. She’s been working this family business, bit by bit, getting herself closer to the money and making sure I’m left out of decisions I should be part of. And when I try to point it out, people act like I’m making too much of it, like it’s just a misunderstanding. But I’ve been watching this for over a year now. I know what I’ve been seeing. It’s not beyond normal means—it’s her being deliberate and sneaky.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period when you felt unusually energetic and needed much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you mean. I’ve had nights where I didn’t sleep well because I was worried or going over things in my head, but that’s not the same as feeling full of energy, you know?\n\nI’m 52, I get tired like anybody else. If I don’t sleep, I usually feel it the next day. I’m not up cleaning the whole house at three in the morning feeling wonderful or anything like that. I can get worked up when I’m upset, but I wouldn’t say I’ve had a period where I suddenly needed hardly any sleep and had all this extra energy.\n\nDoctor: Apart from your sister-in-law, do you feel that other people are also deliberately trying to harm you or work against you?\n\nPatient: No, not really other people, not in the same way. I mean, people may be going along with her, or they may be letting her explain things her way, and that upsets me because it makes me look like I’m the difficult one. But the person I came here about is my husband’s sister.\n\nShe’s the one who’s been working this family business, little by little, getting herself in closer to the money and the decisions, and pushing me out like I don’t have a say. And it’s not something that happened once, you know? It’s been over a year of small things, and then when I say something, folks try to smooth it over like, “Oh, that’s just how she is,” or “You’re reading too much into it.” But I know what I’ve been seeing.\n\nSo no, I’m not saying everybody is out to get me. I’m saying she is doing this deliberately, and other people just don’t want to admit it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself becoming much less interested in being around people or doing things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I wouldn’t say I’ve lost interest in people or stopped doing the things I usually do. I still go to my quilting group, and I still go to choir practice. I’ve been keeping up with my GED lessons too, and I do my senior walks like I normally do. Those things are important to me, and I haven’t just dropped them or anything.\n\nWhat has changed is more around family, especially anything where my husband’s sister is going to be there. I’m more careful now. I don’t feel comfortable at every gathering the way I used to, because I don’t trust her, and I don’t like sitting there pretending everything is fine when I know what she’s been doing. So I might avoid certain family things, or I might not stay as long, depending on who’s there and what it’s about.\n\nBut that’s not me not wanting to be around anybody or not enjoying my life. I still do my regular activities. It’s mostly changed how much I trust her and which family gatherings feel comfortable for me.\n\nDoctor: Before all this with your sister-in-law began, did you generally tend to distrust other people and suspect they had hidden motives?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I was like that before, no. I mean, I’ve never been someone who just trusts everybody right away, but I think that’s normal, isn’t it? You use your judgment. If somebody seems pushy or two-faced, you notice it. But I wasn’t walking around thinking people had hidden motives all the time.\n\nBefore this, I got along with people pretty well. I did my things, talked to people, went where I needed to go. I might have had little disagreements here and there, like anyone does, but I wasn’t sitting there studying everybody and thinking they were against me.\n\nThis has been different because of how it’s happened and how it keeps happening. So I wouldn’t say I generally distrusted people before. I’d say I was careful, maybe, but not suspicious of everybody.\n\nDoctor: For how long have you been certain that your sister-in-law is deliberately trying to push you out and take control of the money?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s been about a year and two months now. It started back when the arguments with my husband’s sister began over the family care planning, and that’s when I first really saw what she was doing.\n\nAt first, I tried to tell myself maybe it was just a disagreement, maybe she was just being pushy, but over time it became clearer to me. It wasn’t just one argument or one bad day. It’s been going on since then, little by little, and by now I feel certain she’s been trying to push me out and get control over the money side of things.\n\nDoctor: Do other people ever tell you that your speech is hard to follow or that you jump from one idea to another?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think people usually tell me that. I mean, sometimes my family might say I’m going on too long about something, but that’s different, I think. I try to explain myself clearly, especially when something matters to me, and maybe I give a lot of background because I don’t want people misunderstanding what I mean.\n\nBut I haven’t had people say they can’t follow me, or that I’m jumping all over the place. Not that I can remember. I might circle back to a point if I feel like it didn’t come out right the first time, but in my mind it all connects. So, no, I haven’t really noticed that being a problem.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

No, not through the television or radio or the internet like that. I don’t think the TV is talking to me or anything.

And everybody keeps trying to make it sound softer, like, “Oh, she didn’t mean it that way,” or “That’s just how families are,” but after more than a year of watching it, I know what I’ve been seeing. It’s deliberate. She knows exactly what she’s doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing voices when no one else is there?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Not voices like someone talking to me when nobody’s there, no. I mean, sometimes I’ll be thinking things over in my head, going back over conversations, but that’s just me thinking. I’m not hearing some voice in the room or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Has your sister-in-law done anything that felt impossible or beyond normal means, like influencing your thoughts or controlling events in a way ordinary people couldn’t?\n\nPatient: No, not like… magic or controlling my thoughts, if that’s what you mean. I’m not saying she has some strange power over me or over the house.\n\nWhat I’m saying is more ordinary than that, but that doesn’t make it less real. She’s been working this family business, bit by bit, getting herself closer to the money and making sure I’m left out of decisions I should be part of. And when I try to point it out, people act like I’m making too much of it, like it’s just a misunderstanding. But I’ve been watching this for over a year now. I know what I’ve been seeing. It’s not beyond normal means—it’s her being deliberate and sneaky.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period when you felt unusually energetic and needed much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you mean. I’ve had nights where I didn’t sleep well because I was worried or going over things in my head, but that’s not the same as feeling full of energy, you know?\n\nI’m 52, I get tired like anybody else. If I don’t sleep, I usually feel it the next day. I’m not up cleaning the whole house at three in the morning feeling wonderful or anything like that. I can get worked up when I’m upset, but I wouldn’t say I’ve had a period where I suddenly needed hardly any sleep and had all this extra energy.\n\nDoctor: Apart from your sister-in-law, do you feel that other people are also deliberately trying to harm you or work against you?\n\nPatient: No, not really other people, not in the same way. I mean, people may be going along with her, or they may be letting her explain things her way, and that upsets me because it makes me look like I’m the difficult one. But the person I came here about is my husband’s sister.\n\nShe’s the one who’s been working this family business, little by little, getting herself in closer to the money and the decisions, and pushing me out like I don’t have a say. And it’s not something that happened once, you know? It’s been over a year of small things, and then when I say something, folks try to smooth it over like, “Oh, that’s just how she is,” or “You’re reading too much into it.” But I know what I’ve been seeing.\n\nSo no, I’m not saying everybody is out to get me. I’m saying she is doing this deliberately, and other people just don’t want to admit it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself becoming much less interested in being around people or doing things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I wouldn’t say I’ve lost interest in people or stopped doing the things I usually do. I still go to my quilting group, and I still go to choir practice. I’ve been keeping up with my GED lessons too, and I do my senior walks like I normally do. Those things are important to me, and I haven’t just dropped them or anything.\n\nWhat has changed is more around family, especially anything where my husband’s sister is going to be there. I’m more careful now. I don’t feel comfortable at every gathering the way I used to, because I don’t trust her, and I don’t like sitting there pretending everything is fine when I know what she’s been doing. So I might avoid certain family things, or I might not stay as long, depending on who’s there and what it’s about.\n\nBut that’s not me not wanting to be around anybody or not enjoying my life. I still do my regular activities. It’s mostly changed how much I trust her and which family gatherings feel comfortable for me.\n\nDoctor: Before all this with your sister-in-law began, did you generally tend to distrust other people and suspect they had hidden motives?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I was like that before, no. I mean, I’ve never been someone who just trusts everybody right away, but I think that’s normal, isn’t it? You use your judgment. If somebody seems pushy or two-faced, you notice it. But I wasn’t walking around thinking people had hidden motives all the time.\n\nBefore this, I got along with people pretty well. I did my things, talked to people, went where I needed to go. I might have had little disagreements here and there, like anyone does, but I wasn’t sitting there studying everybody and thinking they were against me.\n\nThis has been different because of how it’s happened and how it keeps happening. So I wouldn’t say I generally distrusted people before. I’d say I was careful, maybe, but not suspicious of everybody.\n\nDoctor: For how long have you been certain that your sister-in-law is deliberately trying to push you out and take control of the money?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s been about a year and two months now. It started back when the arguments with my husband’s sister began over the family care planning, and that’s when I first really saw what she was doing.\n\nAt first, I tried to tell myself maybe it was just a disagreement, maybe she was just being pushy, but over time it became clearer to me. It wasn’t just one argument or one bad day. It’s been going on since then, little by little, and by now I feel certain she’s been trying to push me out and get control over the money side of things.\n\nDoctor: Do other people ever tell you that your speech is hard to follow or that you jump from one idea to another?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think people usually tell me that. I mean, sometimes my family might say I’m going on too long about something, but that’s different, I think. I try to explain myself clearly, especially when something matters to me, and maybe I give a lot of background because I don’t want people misunderstanding what I mean.\n\nBut I haven’t had people say they can’t follow me, or that I’m jumping all over the place. Not that I can remember. I might circle back to a point if I feel like it didn’t come out right the first time, but in my mind it all connects. So, no, I haven’t really noticed that being a problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed that someone was sending you special messages through the television, radio, or internet?\n\nPatient: No, not through the television or radio or the internet like that. I don’t think the TV is talking to me or anything.\n\nWhat I’m saying is more direct than that. I came here because my husband’s sister has been working things in the family business and the money side, trying to get control and push me out of decisions. People try to smooth it over, like, “Oh, she didn’t mean it that way,” or “Don’t make it into something,” but I know what I’ve been seeing. This has been going on for more than a year now, and it’s not some message hidden in a program or online. It’s what she’s doing right in front of everybody, only they don’t want to call it what it is.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

Subtypes: Persecutory Delusions, Referential Delusions, Grandiose Delusions, Erotomanic Delusions, Nihilistic Delusions, Somatic Delusions, Jealous Delusions\n[S020] Hallucinations: Perception-like experiences that occur without an external stimulus and are as vivid and clear as normal perception. Subtypes: Derailment or Loose Associations, Tangentiality, Incoherence or Word Salad, Neologisms, Clang Associations\n[S022] Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior: A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation.  Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost. Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy. Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt. Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event. Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings). Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.   }

Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nNo, not really other people, not in the same way. I mean, people may be going along with her, or they may be letting her explain things her way, and that upsets me because it makes me look like I’m the difficult one. But the person I came here about is my husband’s sister.\n\nShe’s the one who’s been working this family business, little by little, getting herself in closer to the money and the decisions, and pushing me out like I don’t have a say. And it’s not something that happened once, you know? It’s been over a year of small things, and then when I say something, folks try to smooth it over like, “Oh, that’s just how she is,” or “You’re reading too much into it.” But I know what I’ve been seeing.\n\nSo no, I’m not saying everybody is out to get me. I’m saying she is doing this deliberately, and other people just don’t want to admit it.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings). Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nNo, I don’t think people usually tell me that. Not that I can remember. I might circle back to a point if I feel like it didn’t come out right the first time, but in my mind it all connects.
